An innovative fuzzy Revan index with CRITIC integration in multi-criterion decision-making
Authors: Iqra Yaqoot, Zeeshan Saleem Mufti, Gamachu Adugna Ganati
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
This study introduces the fuzzy Revan index (FRI), a new comprehensive index of fuzzy graphs that can be used to facilitate multi-criteria decision-making under uncertainty. Unlike other fuzzy graph indices that only consider local structural properties, the FRI uses membership values for both vertices and edges to identify larger connectivity patterns. We defined its theoretical properties, such as the upper bounds of particular classes of graphs, and implemented a computational algorithm with a pseudocode that can be used for large-scale networks. In this context, the FRI was integrated with a CRITIC-based approach to make decisions, wherein the membership values were calculated using the sigmoid and multiplicative methods. A national highway site selection problem was considered to illustrate the relevance of the proposed approach for real-world applications. Six site locations were evaluated based on a fuzzy graph derived from five weighted criteria: land acquisition price, environmental considerations, geotechnical suitability, accessibility, and future expansion potential. Combining FRI values with a multi-criteria decision-making method provides a more comprehensive site ranking than that obtained using local measurements. The findings indicate how FRI can enhance the quality of evaluation in uncertain and complex settings and its wider application in the field of information science.

Introduction

Classical graph theory is a basic model for representing the relationships between entities in a network. In the real world, however, there is inherent uncertainty and imprecision in systems that cannot be captured using a binary graph. This restriction led to the emergence of fuzzy set theory by Zadeh53, which allowed for the elements to have degrees of membership instead of the binary classification of membership and non-membership. Rosenfeld43 extended this paradigm to fuzzy graphs, introducing fuzzy graphs with vertices and edges having membership values with the consistency condition that an edge’s membership value cannot exceed the minimum value of its vertices. This pioneering work laid the groundwork for many years of research on fuzzy graph theory. The concept of fuzzy graph theory has been extensively developed in recent years. Some researchers have suggested that it is helpful in modelling complex systems with gradual transitions and plays an important role in a variety of areas such as decision making, image processing, social networks, communication networks, and supply chain management23,32,38,40,45.

Topological indices are numerical characteristics of a graph that reflect its structure. In classical graph theory, the Wiener index49 is used to measure the molecular structure, the Zagreb indices18 characterize the molecular branching, and the Randić index39 is a molecular descriptor. A great deal of research has been devoted to studying these indices for their correlations with structural properties and physicochemical characteristics12,15. However, classical topological indices are essentially based on a binary relationship and cannot be applied to model systems that contain uncertainty and ambiguity. To overcome this, fuzzy extensions of classical topological indices have been proposed. Kalathian et al.25 systematically introduced several fuzzy topological measures, such as the fuzzy Wiener index, fuzzy Zagreb indices, fuzzy harmonic index, and fuzzy Randić index. Islam and Pal20,22 presented fuzzy modified first and second Zagreb indices and proved that complete fuzzy graphs are maximizers of the above indices in mathematical chemistry applications. Ahmad and Sabir2 related fuzzy topological indices to multi criteria decision making and demonstrated their applications in ranking alternatives using uncertainty. Recently, Rao et al.41 introduced the Zagreb indices for q-rung orthopair fuzzy graphs and applied them to vaccine selection, where they showed that the results were validated using the rank correlation with PROMETHEE, TOPSIS, and VIKOR. However, all the current fuzzy topological indices suffer from a serious drawback: they are mostly based on local degree properties or pairwise distances and lack extremal degree information to measure structural imbalance. To this end, current fuzzy descriptors do not include a fuzzy degree for extremal structural attributes based on deviation.

To fix structural imbalance, crisp graph theory was introduced by Kulli26 using the Revan index as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(v) = \Delta + \delta - d(v)$$\end{document} and calculating the index as the sum over the edges of r(u) + r(v). Ediz (2020)13 investigated the response of the Revan index to different network topologies, which showed its sensitivity to network balance and centralization. Gutman and Kulli17 provided mathematical descriptions of various graphs. Recently, Narasimhan et al.37 considered a variety of Revan and hyper-Revan indices for hex-derived networks. The Revan index is 0 for graphs in the ideal case and increases as the graph becomes more heterogeneous (contains more high-degree hubs and more low-degree peripheral vertices). The Revan index is useful for vulnerability analysis and identification of critical nodes because of this sensitivity. Although the Revan index is useful for crisp graph analysis, it has never been applied to fuzzy graphs.

The CRITIC method, introduced by Diakoulaki et al.11 is an objective method for determining criteria weights in multi-criteria decision making, and its weights are calculated directly from the decision matrix using variance and correlation. In recent years, fuzzy CRITIC extensions have been developed, such as green supplier selection using the AIOWA-CRITIC method with subjective and objective weights using game theory54. Another study combined spherical fuzzy CRITIC and TOPSIS to prioritize earthquakes, considering earthquake parameters such as urban landscape, road planning, and evacuation plans47. However, no study has combined the CRITIC and fuzzy topological indices.

The choice of a proper topological index to measure the structural imbalance of a fuzzy graph is a complicated problem to solve, which involves many aspects or attributes and requires a systematic decision-making process. In this study, we propose the use of the Fuzzy Revan Index (FRI) as a global structural index, which is an extension of the classical Revan index, to account for uncertainty by assigning vertex and edge membership functions. The FRI is based on a network-wide definition of connectivity patterns and system-level imbalances, in contrast to fuzzy topological indices, which mainly reflect local structural characteristics, and thus is well-suited for complex multi-criteria decision-making applications. The FRI was incorporated into the CRITIC method for objective weight determination, and the overall approach was used in a problem that was applied to a problem of site selection on a highway.

Objectives and highlights

Choosing an appropriate topological index is an important part of the fuzzy graph analysis. The choice of the best site for a highway project is frequently defined by multiple criteria, imprecision, and some degree of uncertainty. Traditional methods are inadequate for addressing these challenges. Therefore, we suggest applying two promising methods: the fuzzy Revan index (FRI) and CRITIC.

This study had the following main objectives: To define the Fuzzy Revan Index (FRI), to derive its mathematical properties and show the FRI being a generalization of the classical Revan index to fuzzy graphs.To develop an efficient computational algorithm that computes the FRI for large-scale fuzzy graphs to make it applicable for real-world decision-making.To incorporate the FRI with the CRITIC method in the objective weight determination in multi-criteria decision-making problems.To illustrate the applicability and effectiveness of the proposed FRI-CRITIC framework with a practical case study of highway site selection problem having six candidate sites and five evaluation criteria in a realistic scenario.To justify the proposed approach by conducting wide-ranging comparative studies with five well-known fuzzy topological indices using Spearman rank correlation and sensitivity analysis.This study aims to achieve these objectives, which can lead to the development of effective methods for measuring structural imbalance in fuzzy graphs and enhance the knowledge about the application of fuzzy topological indices in multi-criteria decision-making processes under uncertainty.

Structure of the paper

In Section “Literature review”, a literature review on fuzzy graph theory, topological indices, the Revan index, and recent progress in the CRITIC method is presented. The basic terminologies related to fuzzy graph theory are presented in Section “Preliminaries”. In Section “Main results”, the Fuzzy Revan Index (FRI), its mathematical properties, vertex deletion contribution analysis, upper bounds for fundamental graph classes, and computational complexity analysis are presented. The integrated methodology of multi-criteria decision-making (FRI-CRITIC) is presented in Section "Integrated FRI-CRITIC methodology for site selection". Section "Integrated FRI-CRITIC approach for MCDM problems" presents a case study of highway site selection. The results, comparative analysis, statistical validation, sensitivity analysis, and superiority of the proposed FRI-CRITIC approach are discussed in Section "Comparative Analysis with Existing Fuzzy Topological Indices". Section “Conclusion” concludes the paper with the limitations and future directions.

Literature review

Fuzzy graph theory was introduced by Zadeh’s53 extension of graph theory to fuzzy sets and further developed by Rosenfeld43, who established the foundational framework for fuzzy graphs. The structural theory of fuzzy graphs was introduced by Mordeson and Nair32 and Mathew and Sunitha29which paved the way of the development of concepts of connectivity and blocks and fuzzy graph operations. There are some more recent developments such as m-polar fuzzy graphs, and q-rung orthopair fuzzy graphs, which were developed to better represent uncertainty5,41,. To overcome these drawbacks, a q-rung orthopair fuzzy set (q-ROFS) was proposed by Yager50 in which vague information can be represented by using intuitionistic fuzzy set and Pythagorean fuzzy set and the condition of ROFS is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^q + \beta ^q \le 1$$\end{document}. Fuzzy graphs have been applied in various domains, including decision-making systems23, image processing38, social networks40, communication networks32, and supply chain management45 they have been applied in various ways. The examples in this book demonstrate the improved modelling capability of fuzzy graph theory for systems where there are gradual changes and indistinct relationships.

Topological indices are quantifiers of the characteristics of graphs. The structure–physicochemical property correlation has been extensively studied for classical graph theoretical indices, such as the Wiener index49, Zagreb indices18 and Randić index39 of graphs. But the classical topological indices are based on binary relationships which are unable to deal with uncertainty. Therefore, some researchers have suggested extensions to fuzzy sets, called fuzzy extensions. Kalathian et al.25 systematically introduced the fuzzy Wiener, fuzzy Zagreb, fuzzy harmonic and fuzzy Randić indices and gave bounds. In their works, Islam and Pal20 introduced the fuzzy modified first Zagreb index, and proved that fuzzy complete graphs are the ones with the maximum value of the indices. This work was extended to the second Zagreb index for fuzzy graphs, and the correlation between the fuzzy Zagreb index and the physicochemical properties of octane isomers was reported in22. Ahmad and Sabir2 have made connections between fuzzy topological indices and multi-criteria decision making, and demonstrated how they can be applied in the ranking of alternatives where uncertainty is present. Ahmad et al.1 applied fuzzy topological indices to cybercrime analysis and discovered the structurally significant vertices of cybercrime networks.

All these fuzzy topological indices share the common element that they take into account aggregated degree/distance measures. Although these indices can be used to measure some structural aspects, they are not specific measures of the distance of each vertex from a network extreme. In the context of decisions, this is especially relevant: In decision making, one has to pay attention to whether there are alternatives that are structurally central or peripheral, in addition to being merely aggregated. The need for a deviation-based imbalance measure that reflects structural heterogeneity is achieved by the proposed Fuzzy Revan Index (FRI). Several studies have been conducted on structural imbalance measures in different graph environments, as shown in Table (1). The types of imbalance measures are discussed along with their applications in various fields in this overview.Table 1Structural imbalance measures in graph theory.ResearchersYearGraph typeApplicationKulli262018Crisp graphStructural imbalance measurementEdiz132020Crisp graphNetwork topology analysisGutman and Kulli172021Crisp graphMathematical characterizationNarasimhan et al.372022Hex-derived networksNetwork vulnerability assessmentFischer et al.142026Transportation infrastructureSustainability and smart systemsAlic et al.62025Highway aerodynamicsCrosswind influence modelingPresent study2026Fuzzy graphMCDM for highway site selection

Table 1Structural imbalance measures in graph theory.ResearchersYearGraph typeApplicationKulli262018Crisp graphStructural imbalance measurementEdiz132020Crisp graphNetwork topology analysisGutman and Kulli172021Crisp graphMathematical characterizationNarasimhan et al.372022Hex-derived networksNetwork vulnerability assessmentFischer et al.142026Transportation infrastructureSustainability and smart systemsAlic et al.62025Highway aerodynamicsCrosswind influence modelingPresent study2026Fuzzy graphMCDM for highway site selection

Structural imbalance measures in graph theory.

The crisp Revan index introduced by Kulli26 is deviation-based imbalance measure characterized by Ediz13 and Gutman and Kulli17 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(v) = \Delta + \delta - d(v)$$\end{document}. However, this index has not been extended to fuzzy graphs yet. The Revan index has the advantage of being able to quantify the difference in the degree of each vertex of the network from the maximum and minimum degrees of the network, which are not measured by the other indices, thus the index can measure the degree of structural difference that exists in the network. The Revan index is zero in a regular graph, that is, a graph where every vertex has the same degree, and the higher the graph is heterogeneous, the more its degree varies from the hubs towards the peripheral ones, the higher this index. The Revan index also has a structural imbalance sensitivity, which can be applied in a variety of scenarios as in the communication network vulnerability assessment or identification of critical nodes in supply chain applications.

The need to use advanced analytic approaches for highway site selection has been emphasized in recent transportation infrastructure planning studies. The authors of14 gave a detailed mini-review on sustainability and smart systems in transportation infrastructure, stating that sustainability should be considered in all aspects of transportation infrastructure planning. They state that the classic approaches tend to mask interdependencies between criteria and make suboptimal decisions. This observation directly contributes to the use of fuzzy graphs as a way to naturally represent interdependencies between alternatives in the membership of the edges. Alic et al.6 constructed numerical models to study the influence of crosswind on the vehicle aerodynamics in highway traffic. Their work shows that there are many interacting physical factors in the design of highways, a point also stressed in the present problem, which involves geotechnical suitability and environmental impact interacting in complex ways. In order to determine the optimal alignment for highways in Tokat region of Turkey, Yakar and Celik51 proposed a three-stage model based on GIS and MCDM, where the western slope of the hill was considered as the best alignment. The data set is also utilized in the present study to validate the proposed FRI-CRITIC framework.

The CRITIC method11 is used to determine objective weights for the criteria. In recent years, extensions of fuzzy CRITIC have emerged, such as the AIOWA-CRITIC method for green supplier selection under spherical fuzzy sets and subjective and objective weight integration under game theory and sensitivity and ablation analysis of the results54. Another study integrated spherical fuzzy CRITIC and TOPSIS methods to prioritize earthquake risks, creating earthquake parameters like urban landscape, road planning, industrial settlements, evacuation plan, energy, and communication networks47. In 2025, the study of medical waste management using f,g,h-fractional fuzzy set-based Hamming distance was proposed7. In the new study in 2025, a new study is added, which involves the spherical fuzzy CRITIC-RATGOS for airport RE investment decisions52. The present study integrates FRI with CRITIC for objective weight determination and to date no study has been conducted that has considered both the CRITIC and fuzzy topological indices. Moreover, CRITIC is very appealing because it does not require subjective weight elicitation by the decision makers which can be expensive, time-consuming, and is subject to cognitive preferences.

Based on the existing literature, this study fills five identified gaps in the literature. First, global contrast/balance measure of fuzzy graphs based on extremal degrees does not exist. Second, there is no link between fuzzy topological indices and objective weight determination methods, such as CRITIC. Third, although fuzzy topological indices have proven to be useful in other applications, to the best of our knowledge, their application in planning transportation infrastructure, specifically for the selection of highway sites with multiple conflicting criteria, was not previously attempted, which is what we are doing in our case study. Fourth, available indices do not provide an index to calculate the importance of a vertex in the context of deletion analysis, which involves examining the network structure without a given vertex. Fifth, there is a lack of rigorous comparisons of the validity of several fuzzy topological indices by widely accepted statistical methods. These contributions enhance the theory of the fuzzy graphs as well as the use of fuzzy topological indices in complex multi-criteria decision-making under uncertainty.

More details about fuzzy graph theory, Topological Indices and Multi-criteria Decision Making can be found in24,29,31,44.

Preliminaries

In this section, we summarize the fundamental terminology and notation required for subsequent development. Throughout this paper, all graphs are assumed to be finite.

Definition 3.9

33 A fuzzy complete graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {K}_n$$\end{document} contains edges between every pair of distinct vertices, with edge memberships bounded above by the minimum of the endpoint memberships:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{\mathfrak {K}}(\mathfrak {xy}) \le \min \{\mu _{\mathfrak {K}}(\mathfrak {x}),\mu _{\mathfrak {K}}(\mathfrak {y})\} \quad \forall \mathfrak {x},\mathfrak {y} \in \mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {K}}.$$\end{document}

Definition 3.10

46 A complete bipartite fuzzy graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {K}_{\mathfrak {m},\mathfrak {n}}$$\end{document} consists of vertex partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Y}$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\mathcal {X}| = \mathfrak {m}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\mathcal {Y}| = \mathfrak {n}$$\end{document}. Every vertex in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document} connects to every vertex in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Y}$$\end{document}, and these connections satisfy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{\mathfrak {K}}(\mathfrak {xy}) \le \min \{\mu _{\mathfrak {K}}(\mathfrak {x}),\mu _{\mathfrak {K}}(\mathfrak {y})\} \quad \forall \mathfrak {x} \in \mathcal {X}, \mathfrak {y} \in \mathcal {Y}.$$\end{document}No edges exist within \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document} or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Y}$$\end{document}.

Definition 3.11

29 A fuzzy star graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {S}_n$$\end{document} contains one central vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {c}$$\end{document} adjacent to n outer vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {w}_1,\mathfrak {w}_2,\ldots ,\mathfrak {w}_n$$\end{document}, and no edges exist among the outer vertices. The membership values satisfy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{\mathfrak {S}}(\mathfrak {c}\mathfrak {w}_i) \le \min \{\mu _{\mathfrak {S}}(\mathfrak {c}),\mu _{\mathfrak {S}}(\mathfrak {w}_i)\} \quad \text {for } i = 1,2,\ldots ,n.$$\end{document}

Definition 3.12

34 A fuzzy graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {G} = (\mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}},\mu _{\mathfrak {G}},\nu _{\mathfrak {G}})$$\end{document} is called strong if each edge attains the maximal permissible membership, namely\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {uv}) = \min \{\mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u}),\mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {v})\} \quad \forall \mathfrak {uv} \in \mathcal {E}_{\mathfrak {G}}.$$\end{document}In such graphs, the edge memberships are completely determined by the vertex membership values.

Fuzzy topological indices

In practical network models, structural relationships are often affected by uncertainty or gradual transitions rather than binary interactions. Fuzzy topological indices extend classical graph invariants by incorporating vertex and edge membership values into their formulations. This extension enables the quantitative evaluation of structural properties in environments where imprecision is inherent to the data.

The conceptual basis of fuzzy graphs was introduced by Rosenfeld43, while further structural investigations were developed by Mathew and Sunitha29. The connection between classical graph invariants and fuzzy structures was systematically studied by Mordeson and Nair31. Additional contributions addressing uncertain and weighted network frameworks can be found in3.

Definition 3.13

25 Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {G} = (\mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}},\mu _{\mathfrak {G}},\nu _{\mathfrak {G}})$$\end{document} be a fuzzy graph. The fuzzy modified first Zagreb index is defined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FZ_1(\mathfrak {G}) = \sum _{\mathfrak {u} \in \mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}}} \left[ \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u})\, d_f(\mathfrak {u}) \right] ^2.$$\end{document}

30 If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {G} = (\mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}},\mu _{\mathfrak {G}},\nu _{\mathfrak {G}})$$\end{document} is connected, the fuzzy Wiener index is expressed as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FW(\mathfrak {G}) = \sum _{\{\mathfrak {u},\mathfrak {v}\} \subseteq \mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}}} \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u})\,\mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {v})\, d_f(\mathfrak {u},\mathfrak {v}),$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_f(\mathfrak {u},\mathfrak {v})$$\end{document} represents the fuzzy distance between vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {u}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {v}$$\end{document}.

Definition 3.16

21 The fuzzy F-index of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {G} = (\mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}},\mu _{\mathfrak {G}},\nu _{\mathfrak {G}})$$\end{document} is computed as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FFI(\mathfrak {G}) = \sum _{\mathfrak {u} \in \mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}}} \left[ \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u})\, d_f(\mathfrak {u}) \right] ^3.$$\end{document}

Definition 3.17

8 The fuzzy hyper-Zagreb index of a fuzzy graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {G} = (\mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}},\mu _{\mathfrak {G}},\nu _{\mathfrak {G}})$$\end{document} is defined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FHM(\mathfrak {G}) = \sum _{\mathfrak {uv} \in \mathcal {E}_{\mathfrak {G}}} \left[ \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u})\,d_f(\mathfrak {u}) + \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {v})\,d_f(\mathfrak {v}) \right] ^2.$$\end{document}

Definition 3.18

8 The fuzzy atom–bond connectivity index of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {G} = (\mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}},\mu _{\mathfrak {G}},\nu _{\mathfrak {G}})$$\end{document} is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FABC(\mathfrak {G}) = \sum _{\mathfrak {uv} \in \mathcal {E}_{\mathfrak {G}}} \sqrt{ \frac{\mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u})\,d_f(\mathfrak {u}) + \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {v})\,d_f(\mathfrak {v}) - 2}{\mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u})\,d_f(\mathfrak {u}) \cdot \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {v})\,d_f(\mathfrak {v})} }.$$\end{document}

Definition 3.21

8 The fuzzy harmonic index of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {G} = (\mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}},\mu _{\mathfrak {G}},\nu _{\mathfrak {G}})$$\end{document} is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FH(\mathfrak {G}) = \sum _{\mathfrak {uv} \in \mathcal {E}_{\mathfrak {G}}} \frac{2}{\mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u})\,d_f(\mathfrak {u}) + \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {v})\,d_f(\mathfrak {v})}.$$\end{document}

Main results

In this section, we derive several theoretical bounds for the fuzzy Revan index corresponding to fundamental graph families, such as paths, cycles, complete bipartite graphs, and star graphs. In addition, a systematic computational approach for evaluating the fuzzy Revan index is provided, along with its associated upper bounds.

Vertex membership value

In fuzzy modeling, the sigmoid function converts vertex attributes into membership values within [0, 1], enabling smooth transitions between low and high-membership levels35,48. For a vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {u}$$\end{document}, the membership value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u})$$\end{document} is defined by1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\textbf{u}) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-(\alpha x_i - \beta )}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i$$\end{document} denotes the selected attribute of the vertex, typically its degree19. Vertices with larger attribute values receive membership values approaching one (stronger participation), whereas those with smaller values receive values near zero (weaker involvement)10,36.

Edge membership value

The determination of the edge membership values is based on the interaction strength between the corresponding vertices. One widely-used method is to compute the edge membership according to the product of the membership degrees of the endpoints as described in4. This multiplicative representation captures the multiplicative nature of the intensity of a connection, where both vertices are involved in the connection28. If v and u are two vertices of G then2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {uv}) = \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u}) \cdot \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {v}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Parameter influence on membership values

The effect of the parameters α and β in Equation (1) on the vertex and edge memberships are illustrated in Fig. 2. The parameter α determines the steepness of the membership curve or the transition rate between low and high membership regions, while β introduces horizontal shifts, which change the threshold between low and high membership regions9,42.Fig. 2Variation of the membership value for the parameters α (steepness) and β (horizontal shift). The various curves are for varying α values, and have a fixed value of β = 2.5.

Variation of the membership value for the parameters α (steepness) and β (horizontal shift). The various curves are for varying α values, and have a fixed value of β = 2.5.

Fuzzy Revan index

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {G} = (\mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}}, \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}, \nu _{\mathfrak {G}})$$\end{document} be a connected fuzzy graph whose vertex membership function is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{\mathfrak {G}}$$\end{document}, and edge membership function is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{\mathfrak {G}}$$\end{document}. The edge set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {G}$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_{\mathfrak {G}} = \{\mathfrak {u}\mathfrak {v}: \nu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u}\mathfrak {v})> 0\}$$\end{document}. A fuzzy degree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_f(\mathfrak {u})$$\end{document} for each vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {u} \in \mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}}$$\end{document} is defined, following Definition 3.6.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _f(\mathfrak {G}) = \max \{d_f(\mathfrak {u}): \mathfrak {u} \in \mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}}\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _f(\mathfrak {G}) = \min \{d_f(\mathfrak {u}): \mathfrak {u} \in \mathcal {V}_{\mathfrak {G}}\}$$\end{document} represent the maximum and minimum fuzzy degrees, respectively. The fuzzy Revan degree for a vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {u}$$\end{document} is thus defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u}) = \Delta _f(\mathfrak {G}) + \delta _f(\mathfrak {G}) - d_f(\mathfrak {u}).$$\end{document}The fuzzy Revan index of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {G}$$\end{document}, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FRI(\mathfrak {G})$$\end{document}, is defined by the summation across all edges:3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} FRI(\mathfrak {G}) = \sum _{\mathfrak {u}\mathfrak {v} \in \mathcal {E}_{\mathfrak {G}}} \left[ \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u}) \, r_{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {u}) + \mu _{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {v}) \, r_{\mathfrak {G}}(\mathfrak {v}) \right] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The FRI value measures the degree of imbalance in a fuzzy network. The higher the FRI, the more centralized the network, and the lower the FRI, the more balanced it will be. This simplifies the process of identifying critical and peripheral alternatives for decision-makers. Table 2 provides a practical interpretation of the FRI values.Table 2How FRI values are interpreted in practice.InterpretationFRI rangePractical meaningVery high imbalance> 9.0Structurally critical; removal drastically changes networkImbalance8.0 - 9.0Important role in network connectivityModerate imbalance7.0 - 8.0Moderate imbalanceLow imbalance6.0 - 7.0Somewhat peripheralMinimal imbalance< 6.0Peripheral; minimal impact if removed
